YouTube’s biggest creators join forces to support the Susan Wojcicki Foundation’s critical mission: changing the conversation about lung cancer

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In August 2024, YouTube‘s former longtime CEO, Susan Wojcicki, passed away at age 56. She battled non-small-cell lung cancer for two years and left behind one heck of a legacy.

Now Susan’s husband Dennis Troper and her sisters, Dr. Janet Wojcicki and Anne Wojcicki, have teamed up with professionals in clinical research, AI, diagnostics, and early detection to launch the Susan Wojcicki Foundation, which wants to “change the conversation” around lung cancer and help more people access screening, early detection, and prevention.

And, fittingly, the SWF is marking Lung Cancer Awareness Month by telling people about its mission on YouTube–with the help of creators.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

MrBeast, Mark Rober, Hank Green, Lilly Singh, Cassey Ho, Marina Mogilko, Cleo Abram, Mark Vins, Jake Roper, sWooZie, Matthew & Stephanie Patrick, Dr. Mike, Stephanie Chuang, and Rhett & Link all joined the SWF’s first YouTube video, offering their advice about how it can most effectively connect with viewers and share its message.

“The channel and its contents will address the fact that anyone with lungs can get lung cancer, and nobody deserves it,” Audrey Eatherly, the SWF’s Head of Creator and Community Partnerships, tells Tubefilter. “Early detection and prevention are key. But stigma and confusion keep too many people from talking about it or asking about a screening.”

The widespread assumption that only smokers can get lung cancer and the societal propensity to blame people who get it is a critical challenge to helping them, she adds. Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer deaths worldwide, killing more people each year than leukemia, lymphoma, and breast and prostate cancers combined.

“And yet, it receives the least amount of funding per death,” she says. “The stigma and misunderstanding have been barriers to addressing this critical challenge.”

Yvonne Diaz, a co-founder & Chair @ Oncogene Cancer Research and Board Member @ ALK Positive who has stage 4 ALK+ lung cancer, puts it well: “No matter how you wound up with lung cancer, you deserve the best outcomes, the best care, the best support, and empathy.”

Eatherly says the SWF wanted to bring its mission home to YouTube because the platform has the power to “transform lung cancer research into meaningful human conversations, bringing experts, research, real stories, shared experiences, and understanding to the forefront.”

The channel will share “credible, science-backed information and recent advancements” through things like deep-dive interviews with experts, informative Shorts, FAQs, and creator collaborations, she says.

Speaking of creators, they aren’t just helping launch the SWF’s YouTube channel. Jake Roper, Mark Vins (aka Brave Wilderness), sWooZie, and Matthew & Stephanie Patrick are all on the foundation’s official Creator Advisory Council.

“As longtime creators on YouTube, Matthew and I recognize the massive impact the platform has had on the world and how Susan Wojcicki’s leadership shaped its growth for almost a decade,” the Patricks tell Tubefilter. “The Susan Wojcicki Foundation carries Susan’s legacy forward through the very platform she helped build, and the creators whose careers she made possible are here to help spread its message. While YouTube is an entertainment platform, for many people it’s also their primary source of information and education online, making it a powerful place for the Susan Wojcicki Foundation to share a global message about lung cancer awareness and treatment.”

Stephanie also notes that she has a master’s degree in cancer pharmacology, and has seen firsthand “the massive scientific advancements made in the last decade in cancers that were previously thought to be untreatable.”

“The research the Susan Wojcicki Foundation is supporting is incredibly promising and could lead to significant improvements in patient outcomes while accelerating progress in lung cancer care,” she says.

Vins adds that he’s dedicated to the SWF because he wants to honor Susan’s lasting presence in the creator space.

“Susan was revered as a business leader who helped shape the technology and media landscape, but to creators, she was revered for something even more personal—her unwavering belief in our potential,” he says. “She empowered our voices at a time when the possibilities of YouTube were only beginning to emerge. She understood that creators weren’t simply making videos, we were building communities and creating relationships that would make a meaningful impact in the world.

“Ultimately, the greatest way I can honor Susan’s legacy is to do exactly what she always encouraged us to do: speak from the heart, create with integrity, and use our platforms to make a positive difference. Susan believed in the power of creators to change the world. I hope we can continue proving her right to bring greater awareness to lung cancer and support the pursuit of early detection that will save lives.”

You can check out the SWF’s YouTube channel here, and read more about its mission on its website.

The New York Times Company has reportedly “entered exclusive negotiations” to acquire Letterboxd for $330 million, according to a report from The New York Times itself.

Letterboxd, an online platform for movie lovers that could be compared to Goodreads for movies, has grown dramatically in recent years. Letterboxd reported 3 million users in January 2021, a figure that grew to 28 million by April of this year. More than half of its users are under 35.

Letterboxd was previously reported to be seeking a new owner. According to the Times, film studios A24 and Sony were interested in the acquisition, but A24 was priced out.

Letterboxd is free, but users can access advanced features with a paid subscription. Its projected earnings for this year are estimated at $15 million, which is only about 4.5% of what the Times is supposedly offering to buy the company for.

The New York Times Company declined to comment on the acquisition to its own paper (lol). Personally, we wouldn’t be surprised if these anonymous tips were coming from inside the house, so we’re inclined to take this report pretty seriously.

Airbnb is working on an AI travel agent

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The travel company is planning an “AI-native” trip-planning experience that’s set to roll out next year.

The travel company is planning an “AI-native” trip-planning experience that’s set to roll out next year.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Airbnb plans to roll out an AI agent in the first half of 2027 that will help users plan and book travel, the company said in its Q3 2026 earnings shareholder letter released today.

“Today’s AI agents are good at simple, routine tasks, like making a restaurant reservation or scheduling an appointment,” Airbnb said. “But planning a trip on Airbnb is different.” The company argued that its experience matching hosts and guests, its current role in being part of reservations already, and a “new interface” built “specifically for travel” gives it an edge.

Here’s how Airbnb says that new interface will work:

We don’t think a traditional chatbot is the right way to plan a trip. We’re developing a custom-designed, AI-native experience that builds the interface around your question. Rather than a chat window, our agent draws on a library of hundreds of travel-specific interface elements—maps, photos, comparisons, calendars, itineraries—and composes them in real time to match what you’re looking for. Our agent will help guests explore options, make decisions with the people they’re traveling with, and take action directly in Airbnb.

Airbnb co-founder and CEO Brian Chesky has said before that the company plans to launch an agent in 2027, telling the Skift Global Forum that it would have an interface that’s “much richer than a chatbot.”

In its shareholder letter, Airbnb also said that AI is “the best thing to ever happen to Airbnb” and has changed how it develops products and operates.

Yeah, I’ll Try the Red-Light Therapy Vibrator

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s been a big few years for red-light therapy. You can get it in a skin-clearing LED face mask, a hair-growth-stimulating cap, or a sauna blanket you can self-zip into like a burrito, and I’ve cheered on every new format. I just assumed we’d eventually run out of places for it. Oh, but reader, turns out there was one place left.

LELO, the Swedish luxury sex toy brand, just put red LEDs inside a G-spot vibrator called the MONA Spectra, and the brand says you can get the benefits of red-light therapy (collagen! circulation!) along with, you know, the main event.

As someone who reads the ingredient list on a $14 serum before it gets anywhere near my face, I had questions. So yes, I tried the red-light therapy vibrator. Here's my honest review, plus what the science actually says about red-light therapy for your vagina.

At its core, the MONA Spectra is a G-spot vibrator. It borrows the curved silhouette of LELO's longtime MONA line, with a rounded tip angled toward the front wall of the vagina (aka G-spot territory) and soft, body-safe silicone wrapped in twisted ribs for extra texture. It has 10 vibration settings and 16 intensity levels, and it's fully waterproof, so the bath is fair game.

The twist (besides the literal twisted ribs) is a built-in array of red LEDs. Out of the box, it runs red light at 615 to 625 nanometers, and the LELO app unlocks near-infrared, blue and green light, plus extra vibration patterns.

If you've ever bought an LED face mask, you already know the pitch. Red light is thought to help your cells repair themselves, which is why it's become such a skin-care staple, and LELO is applying the same idea to vaginal tissue.

LELO says regular use may support collagen and elastin, tissue elasticity, natural lubrication, and sensitivity. The brand also says the LED array gives off a gentle warmth that can relax the pelvic floor muscles and increase blood flow, which, for the record, your body tends to appreciate during sex anyway.

The pitch, basically, is folding a wellness habit into something you might already be doing.

The short answer: it's promising, but it's early. Most of the research on red light and vaginal health has focused on menopause, when dropping estrogen can lead to dryness and thinner, more sensitive tissue. A 2019 review of the research so far concluded that red-light therapy appears to be safe and may help with those symptoms, but the authors called the evidence preliminary, and none of it was testing an at-home vibrator.

LELO ran its own four-week survey with 37 women, and nearly 8 in 10 of those using it a few times a week reported noticeably more natural moisture, while close to 70% said they felt more confident in their bodies. It's an encouraging start, and bigger studies will tell us more. Until then, LELO itself positions the MONA Spectra as an everyday self-care device rather than a medical one, which is the right way to think about it.

So for now, think of the red light as a nice extra. If you're dealing with dryness or pain during sex, talk to your OB-GYN, who can help you figure out what will actually make a difference.

If you've used a LELO vibrator before, the MONA Spectra will feel familiar. It has the same gently curved shape and soft, smooth silicone as the regular MONA, and the twisted ribs add a little extra texture. The vibrations are what you'd expect from LELO, with enough settings to keep things low-key or turn them all the way up.

The one difference is warmth: with the LEDs on, the toy gets a little warm, which LELO's instructions say is normal.

If you're buying it for the red-light benefits, give it time. LELO's own survey ran for four weeks, and the brand recommends sticking to 15 minutes of light a day.

Yes (which makes the price easier to swallow). The lights are optional, so if the red light ends up being the thing you skip, you still own a high-end G-spot vibrator, and a very good one at that.

From what researchers have seen so far, yes, as long as you're using a device designed for intimate use and following its instructions. The MONA Spectra checks those boxes: it's made of body-safe silicone that meets ISO 3533, the international safety standard for sex toys, and LELO says its LEDs are UV-free, so you're not exposing sensitive skin to the kind of light that causes damage. A few ground rules from LELO:

If you're pregnant or managing a health condition, check with your doctor before adding any light therapy device to your routine, and read LELO's user manual for the full list of warnings.

If you already have a red-light routine and you've been eyeing a nicer G-spot vibrator anyway, the MONA Spectra makes a lot of sense. If you mostly want a great G-spot vibe and the light therapy is a maybe, LELO's regular MONA has the same familiar shape for a lot less money.

Yes. LELO says it's 100% waterproof and safe for the bath or shower.

LELO says the app is anonymous and fully encrypted, and you don't need it to use the toy's basic settings.

$299 is a lot of money for a vibrator, no way around it. It's also in the same price range as plenty of red-light face masks, and none of those double as a sex toy. The MONA Spectra is a seriously good G-spot vibrator, and the red light is a fun bonus for anyone who's already bought into the wellness of it all. If you haven't, stick with a regular LELO vibrator and save the money.

APOD: 2026 November 6 – A Farewell to Swift

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: It is time to say goodbye to NASA's Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory. After nearly 22 years of service, the satellite re-entered Earth's atmosphere on November 4 at 7:39 EST over the North Sumatra region of Indonesia. The featured image is a mosaic created in 2018 using the first one million images captured by the satellite's Ultraviolet/Optical Telescope. Swift had a combination of instruments, including an X-ray telescope and a gamma-ray telescope, that worked together to enable humanity to study, in unprecedented detail, the fastest and most energetic explosions in the universe. The most recent solar maximum accelerated the decay of the satellite's orbit. An innovative mission to rescue Swift by boosting its orbit was not successful. Swift burned on reentry like a shooting star, but the impressive data acquired by the telescope over its mission will continue to bring light to the mysteries of the cosmos for years to come. May it keep inspiring new missions to boldly go into space, explore new data and seek out new science. Tomorrow's picture: what's next?

Meta, Google, and TikTok sue California AG over teen social media law

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta, Google, and TikTok have each filed lawsuits against California Attorney General Rob Bonta in an attempt to block a law that restricts social media features it deems addictive for users under 16.

California Governor Gavin Newsom signed the bill, known as AB 1709, into law in September, and it’s slated to take effect at the start of the new year.

The law prohibits social platforms from offering autoplay videos and personalized feeds to users under 16, and it requires the platforms to delete these users’ accounts if they don’t turn those features off. If platforms do not comply, they could be liable for fines of up to $50,000 per affected user under 16 years old.

Meta claims that AB 1709 violates the First Amendment by overriding its editorial decisions about how to display content, adding that Section 230 preempts the law. TikTok and Google made similar arguments.

“Meta currently organizes its feeds of content to include personalized content recommendations and to automatically play media, [so] the Act would make it unlawful for Meta to disseminate speech to minors altogether, and would require Meta to delete minors’ accounts, unless it changes these practices,” the company wrote in its lawsuit.

In an emailed statement, a TikTok spokesperson added, “AB 1709 is a blatantly unconstitutional restriction on free expression that erodes the long-standing and protected practice of curating content based on user preferences, which book stores, TV networks and streaming services all use.”

AB 1709 follows a trend of legislation around the world that seeks to curb teen social media use in an attempt to improve teen mental health outcomes. However, digital safety experts often caution against these laws because they can inadvertently compromise the privacy of all platform users, including adults, by requiring age verification checks that collect more user data.

“AB 1709 compels platforms to collect even more information from every user, invading everyone’s privacy and frustrating our ability to remain anonymous online,” EFF Associate Director of State Affairs Rindala Alajaji said in a press briefing. “Online surveillance by social media companies is harmful, and yet this bill requires even more of it, further concentrating power in the hands of tech companies rather than actually protecting users.”

Alajaji added that teenagers’ use of social media has been instrumental in enabling impactful digital activism, citing the Parkland shooting survivors’ March for Our Lives and climate activist Greta Thunberg’s global reach.

“As a functional ban on social media for youth under 16, AB 1709 cuts young people off from essential information and experiences, silencing the very voices it claims to protect,” Alajaji said.

These platforms are primed to fight this California law as they navigate similar legal battles in various countries. That’s especially true for Meta, which agreed to pay a settlement of up to $18 billion to resolve children’s safety claims brought by 29 U.S. states.

In December 2025, Australia became the first country to ban social media for users under 16 altogether. France passed a similar law, but its top court struck it down, citing concerns about violations of free expression rights.

These platforms also argue that the law would affect parents’ ability to make decisions about their teens’ internet use through parental controls.

A Google spokesperson noted that autoplay on YouTube is turned off for minors by default, and that parents have the ability to turn off their teens’ access to Shorts altogether.

“We believe parents are best positioned to make the right decisions for their families, which is why we offer a range of protections and tools that put parents in control,” a Google spokesperson said. “Overly broad laws like this actually break the systems we’ve built to help kids find high-quality, age-appropriate videos.”

A representative for Attorney General Bonta said that his office is reviewing the filings and is committed to “defending California’s commonsense laws to protect kids online.”

Premier League: 10 things to look out for this weekend

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Lampard appreciation at Everton, Glasner sparking Forest fire, Zubimendi and Kroupi in spotlight and Carrick v Emery

Nothing better encapsulates the dysfunction that was Everton under Farhad Moshiri than the idea that an unknown vandal with a can of spray paint could influence the club’s managerial direction. “Pereira out, Lampard in” they daubed on a wall at Goodison Park when it appeared Vítor Pereira was in line to replace Rafael Benítez in 2022. Alarmed by fan protests, the Everton board opted instead for Frank Lampard and, while his tenure ended 12 months later with the club immersed in another relegation battle, there will be appreciation for the Coventry manager when he makes a first visit to Hill Dickinson Stadium on Friday. The connection between Lampard and Evertonians was genuine and a factor in keeping the club up in 2022. Both parties will be glad to see the other emerging stronger from those troubled times. Jack Grealish, meanwhile, will again be absent for Everton after his recent disciplinary breach, although the manager, David Moyes, said: “We will be working again with Jack soon.” Andy Hunter

Everton v Coventry City, Friday 8pm (all times GMT)

Back in September, when Leeds thrashed Newcastle 4-1 at Elland Road, talk of European qualification filled the West Yorkshire airwaves. Since then Daniel Farke’s side have drawn three and lost two of their ensuing Premier League games, scoring just two goals along the way. Given that their final pass has persistently let them down, optimism is turning to pessimism. Farke would call it realism. The German – who could probably do without facing Roberto De Zerbi’s improving Tottenham on Saturday – is confident his side are making progress but knows they cannot expect to run before properly mastering the art of walking. “We don’t have the individual quality [of the Premier League’s leading teams],” said Farke on Thursday. “We have to compensate by being tactically spot on and good at winning second balls. It’s not a criticism of my players, it’s reality. The dream of Europe is allowed but we know our main aim is to reach 40 points as quickly as possible.” Louise Taylor

Martín Zubimendi has yet to start a Premier League game this season after falling down the pecking order at Arsenal and the Spain midfielder is likely to be the subject of speculation that he could seek to depart in January. The emergence of Myles Lewis-Skelly as an alternative in the No 6 position at the end of last season has had an impact on Zubimendi after he had been a regular starter for most of his debut campaign in the Premier League. The 27-year-old signed from Real Sociedad for around £55m 15 months ago and Mikel Arteta has insisted he remains an integral part of his plans. But with the Arsenal manager trying to find the right balance in his midfield after the arrival of Bruno Guimarães, Zubimendi will be hoping he gets another chance to stake his claim against Hull on Saturday after starting in the narrow win over Slavia Prague in midweek. Ed Aarons

Álvaro Arbeloa is proof that a struggling young manager working in England for the first time can bounce back after a difficult start. Arbeloa was sliding towards the sack a month ago but he has responded at Fulham, who have taken seven points from their last three games, and will hope to extend the unbeaten run when Matthias Jaissle’s inconsistent Newcastle visit Craven Cottage. It is an inviting fixture for Fulham and a big one for Newcastle, who were poor when they lost to Everton on Monday. Jaissle, hired as Eddie Howe’s replacement in the summer, is already under pressure. But the 38-year-old could take inspiration from how Arbeloa has given himself breathing space at Fulham. Jacob Steinberg

Oliver Glasner has previous. He led Wolfsburg from mid-table to the Champions League but fell out with the club hierarchy and left in 2021. Glasner next guided Eintracht Frankfurt to Europa League glory but was openly critical of the squad’s imbalance and quality in four separate outbursts and departed in 2023. His time at Crystal Palace was a great success but after accusing the Eagles’ board in January of “abandoning” his team with the sale of Marc Guéhi to Manchester City, the Austrian left this summer for Nottingham Forest, where he became the fifth head coach in less than a year. You can probably guess where this is going. After Saturday’s defeat by Brentford left Forest 19th, Glasner was typically abrasive: “If the players buy in [to my methods], I think we can have a turnaround. And if not, the sixth manager will come in.” We’ll soon find out if Glasner has won the players around with City visiting Nottingham this weekend. If Forest’s woeful form continues, Glasner may either walk or get his marching orders from trigger-happy owner Evangelos Marinakis. Michael Butler

Nottingham Forest v Manchester City, 5.30pm

Pierre Sage pulled no punches in his assessment of Eddie Nketiah’s career after his excellent winning goal against Fulham last week in the EFL Cup. “I think his potential is higher than his performance for the moment,” said the Crystal Palace manager. “And we have to reduce this gap – because he needs it, the team needs it.” Nketiah is now 27 and won his solitary senior cap for England in 2023 after becoming the record scorer for the under-21s while he was still at Arsenal. His chances of adding to that look unlikely these days having been relegated to third-choice striker at Selhurst Park behind Jean-Philippe Mateta and Jørgen Strand Larsen. But with Mateta suspended for three matches after Palace’s appeal over his red card against Tottenham was rejected, Sunday’s meeting with Liverpool could be another chance for Nketiah to show his ability after scoring a late winner against them in Palace’s thrilling win over Arne Slot’s side last year. EA

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

It has been three weeks since Brighton’s fans were savouring a famous 5-0 thrashing of arch-rivals Crystal Palace at the Amex. But the quirks of the fixture list meant that Fabian Hürzeler’s side played five successive games on the road since then, including two trips to face Manchester City. While they lost some of the momentum from the Palace victory in the defeat by Liverpool at Anfield, last week’s Premier League win over City showed they are serious contenders for the top four and perhaps even more. But Brentford’s visit to the south coast should provide a real test of those credentials as the owner, Tony Bloom, renews his rivalry with his west London counterpart Matthew Benham, who traditionally eschews his seat in the directors’ box for one in the away section. EA

Eli Junior Kroupi did not take the headlines in Bournemouth’s win over Leeds but his cheeky nutmeg and sumptuous no-look pass to set up Amine Adli for the Cherries’ second goal was a reminder of why the youngster continues to be linked with a move to Arsenal, Bayern and PSG, despite being sidelined recently. Kroupi scored 13 Premier League goals last season – only Ryan Giggs, Wayne Rooney, Michael Owen and Romelu Lukaku have reached that total as teenagers – and only Erling Haaland and Will Osula enjoyed a better goal-per-game ratio in 2025-26. Kroupi’s foot injury, sustained in pre-season, has been carefully managed but after three substitute appearances in the wins over Manchester United, Aston Villa and Leeds, will Marco Rose be tempted to start Kroupi at Ipswich? Evanilson is in red hot form as the central striker but Kroupi is just as comfortable as a No 10 and surely has to play if he is fit as Bournemouth chase a seventh consecutive win in all competitions. It is not quite six months since Sunderland beat Chelsea 2-1 at the Stadium of Light and qualified for Europe for the first time in 53 years. A day when Régis Le Bris’s side eclipsed the Club World Cup holders and Enzo Le Fée upstaged Enzo Fernández concluded with Sunderland finishing seventh. During the prolonged post-match celebrations, the captain, Granit Xhaka, grabbed a microphone and declared: “This is just the beginning – we want more.” Fast forward to gloomy November and it is not quite working out like that with even the excitement of next month’s trip to Milan overshadowed by anxiety. Sunderland are bottom of the Premier League, Le Bris concedes his side are in a relegation fight and Xhaka has looked a shadow of his former self ever since Chelsea tried and failed to sign him in the summer. Is Xhaka capable of turning back time? Sunderland securing just a second league win of the season depends on it. Louise Taylor

Can Manchester United win two games of football on the bounce for the first time this season? Being able to pose this question sums up the state Michael Carrick and his side are in as they seek to build on Tuesday’s chaotic comeback victory over Roma. Aston Villa arrive five points better off than their hosts and are everything United are not: stable, upwardly mobile and boasting a manager in Unai Emery who consistently improves players and yields results. As the international break beckons after this last Premier League fixture of the weekend, will Carrick survive what could be an eighth loss in 15 matches? Possibly, if it is not another humiliation, but the truth may be that even United’s erratic executives do not currently know the answer. Jamie Jackson

Taking a Gander at Utah’s Goosenecks

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The San Juan River has carved deep, improbably curvy meanders into the desert landscape.

Tens of millions of years ago, the San Juan River in what is now southern Utah shared some characteristics with today's Mississippi River. In a bygone geologic era, the San Juan flowed across a relatively flat plain that formed in an ancient marine environment. Unencumbered by rugged terrain, the river was free to take a long, meandering path on its gradual journey downslope.

But this part of the San Juan changed when geologic forces pushed the Colorado Plateau upward. Some experts think that within the later stages of this “still-enigmatic” history, starting around 20 million years ago, the plateau’s rise elevated the San Juan’s headwaters, which increased the river’s gradient and sped its flow. With that, the water’s erosive power intensified, and the river started carving its winding path deeper and deeper through 300-million-year-old rock.

Today, on its westward path to Lake Powell, the San Juan River flows through an impressive series of entrenched meanders, also known as “goosenecks.” An astronaut aboard the International Space Station photographed the stark desert topography on October 6, 2026.

In Utah’s Goosenecks State Park, the river runs through a canyon 1,000 feet (300 meters) deep. In places, it nearly loops back on itself, making horseshoe-shaped bends. One section is so curvy that the river travels 5.5 miles (8.9 kilometers) between two points that are only 1.3 miles (2.1 kilometers) apart as the crow—nay, goose—flies.

While the goosenecks are an unusual geologic wonder to behold by day, the area’s dark skies are a spectacle by night. Goosenecks State Park’s remote, high-desert location and 360-degree views of the sky helped earn it an International Dark Sky Park designation in 2021. According to DarkSky International, features such as the Double Cluster in Perseus and the Messier 24 star cloud in the Sagittarius spiral arm of the Milky Way are visible to the unaided eye from the park. In November 2026, dark skies offer the chance to view several meteor showers, with the Northern Taurids, Southern Taurids, and Leonids reaching their peaks throughout the month.

Astronaut photograph ISS075-E-188830 was acquired on October 6, 2026, with a Nikon Z9 digital camera using a focal length of 400 millimeters. It is provided by the ISS Crew Earth Observations Facility and the Earth Science and Remote Sensing Unit at NASA Johnson Space Center. The image was taken by a member of the Expedition 75 crew. The image has been cropped and enhanced to improve contrast, and lens artifacts have been removed. The International Space Station Program supports the laboratory as part of the ISS National Lab to help astronauts take pictures of Earth that will be of the greatest value to scientists and the public, and to make those images freely available on the internet. Additional images taken by astronauts and cosmonauts can be viewed at the NASA/JSC Gateway to Astronaut Photography of Earth. Photo by Bob Wick (BLM), via Flickr. Story by Lindsey Doermann.

ChainLink (LINK), XRP, Monero (XMR) and Zcash (ZEC) Price Analysis For November 6: Sudden Surge of Bullishness Is Here

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ocarina Of Time's Ganondorf Called "One Of The Biggest Miscasts In Gaming"

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

Matt Mercer is one of the world's most accomplished voice actors. He's held dozens of roles across TV, film, and games, appearing in series like Attack on Titan, Resident Evil, Persona, Fire Emblem, Death Stranding, Baldur's Gate, Like A Dragon, and Final Fantasy.

He earns near-universal praise for every role he steps into, but one has been a little more divisive: The Legend of Zelda's Ganondorf.

He first appeared as the character in 2023's The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom, reprising the role in Hyrule Warriors: Age of Imprisonment, and most recently in the Switch 2 remake of Ocarina of Time. The TotK performance drew some criticism from long-time The Legend of Zelda fans, and that criticism has resurfaced now that Ocarina of Time is out.

If you look back at online Tears of the Kingdom discussions, you'll see that Ganondorf's voice was a slight bone of contention. Many cited him as their least favorite version of the character. With Ocarina of Time now out in the public, and Mercer returning as its primary villain, debate has begun to rage again, with many feeling he's not right for the role.

One such discussion is taking place on ResetEra, where Mercer's Ganondorf was called "One of the biggest miscasts in gaming." While that particular phrasing was deemed harsh, there was plenty of criticism of the actor's return.

"I always imagined Dorf to have a very deep, likely accented voice. When he talks in these newer games, it breaks the immersion so much," reads a further snippet from the original post. This was an opinion shared by others.

Zekes said, "Love Matt Mercer, but he just doesn't have enough bass." Boopers added, "I agree, actually. Not a fan of his Ganondorf voice. But eh, whatever, it's not like there's too much of a character there anyway."

Love Matt Mercer, but he just doesn't have enough bass.

The discussion around Mercer's performance on ResetEra became quite nuanced. While some blamed the actor, others blamed Nintendo's voice direction. "BotW and TotK have some of the worst voice direction in recent memory," posted Lumination, with Papercuts adding, "BotW/TotK just have horrific dubs in general regardless of talent."

The discussion was also continued on Reddit once Mercer was revealed for the role, and the reception there was similar. "I'm surprised [Mercer was cast]; it's really generic American voice acting," one commenter said.

If his role as Ganondorf doesn't do it for you, you won't have to wait long for his next appearance, with Mercer returning in Final Fantasy 7 Revelation as Vincent Valentine.

The Problem American Airlines Ran Into When It Inherited The World's 1st Airbus A321neo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Robin Guess | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

American Airlines operates one of the largest Airbus A321neo fleets in the world, using the narrowbody aircraft to serve a variety of domestic and international routes. However, not every A321neo in its fleet is quite the same. Among its aircraft are 10 examples inherited from Alaska Airlines, which originally acquired them following its takeover of Virgin America. Although these aircraft offer similar capabilities to American's other A321neos, their original design has created an unusual fleet-management challenge.

The story goes back to 2017, when Virgin became the first airline to receive an A321neo. Several years and two airline ownership changes later, some of the aircraft eventually found their way into American's fleet. The problem is that they were built before Airbus introduced a significant cabin-design change, meaning American's cannot configure them in exactly the same way as its newer A321neos. This difference has also created an unexpected complication for passengers selecting their seats.

The A321neo represented an important development for the European manufacturer, bringing improved fuel efficiency, reduced emissions, and lower noise levels to the largest member of its A320neo family. Virgin became the launch operator of the aircraft, receiving the first example at a delivery ceremony in Hamburg, Germany, in April 2017. The aircraft was powered by CFM International LEAP-1A engines. At the time, Airbus highlighted the aircraft's ability to deliver fuel savings of at least 15% compared with previous-generation models. It also offered greater passenger capacity and longer range, making it an attractive option for airlines looking to improve the economics of their short- and medium-haul operations. Virgin planned to use the aircraft to expand its existing Airbus fleet while maintaining its distinctive onboard experience. The first aircraft was configured with 185 seats and was expected to enter commercial service on May 31, 2017, operating its inaugural flight between San Francisco International Airport (SFO) and Ronald Reagan Washington National Airport (DCA).

Its cabin included Virgin's familiar premium-oriented features, including multiple service classes and personal entertainment systems. The delivery was particularly significant because it marked the arrival of the first A321neo anywhere in the world. However, Virgin's relationship with the aircraft would not last independently for very long. Alaska Air Group had announced its acquisition of Virgin in April 2016, completing the transaction in December that year. The subsequent integration brought the two airlines together under the Alaska brand, creating a new chapter for Virgin's aircraft and outstanding orders.

Following the acquisition, Alaska inherited Virgin's aircraft and commitments, including its A321neo order. The aircraft were therefore no longer being introduced into an independent Virgin fleet, as originally intended. Instead, they became part of Alaska's broader operations, although the transition did not mean that every aircraft immediately entered service with the acquiring airline. Virgin had originally ordered 10 A321neos, and the aircraft subsequently became part of the complicated fleet transition following the acquisition. Only four of the 10 aircraft eventually transferred to American had actually operated for Virgin. This is an important distinction because the aircraft's original configuration was determined before the later ownership changes took place.

American ultimately took these 10 aircraft from Alaska, adding them to its growing Airbus narrowbody fleet. The transfer provided American with additional capacity without requiring it to introduce an entirely new aircraft family. Nevertheless, the inherited aircraft came with a design characteristic that distinguished them from the A321neos that the oneworld carrier had ordered directly from Airbus.

The aircraft were among the earliest A321neos produced, meaning they predated Airbus' Cabin Flex arrangement. Consequently, although they shared the A321neo name and many of the same underlying characteristics, their cabin layouts were fundamentally different. This would become particularly important when American began integrating them into its own passenger operations.

The A321neo has a larger capacity than the smaller A320, allowing American to transport more people without relying on wide bodies.

The central issue concerns Airbus Cabin Flex, a redesigned exit-door arrangement introduced to provide airlines with greater flexibility when configuring the A321's cabin. Rather than relying entirely on the older arrangement of four pairs of exits, the updated design repositioned certain emergency exits, including the overwing exits, to create additional opportunities for seat installation. The earlier aircraft inherited from Alaska retained the original arrangement, featuring eight exits in total, with four on each side of the fuselage. This was broadly comparable to the configuration used on the older A321ceo. While perfectly suitable from an operational and safety perspective, it limited how the airline could arrange seats compared with the newer Cabin Flex-equipped aircraft. The distinction matters because the position of emergency exits influences the number of seats that can legally and practically be installed in an aircraft cabin. Cabin Flex allowed airlines to make better use of available cabin space, supporting higher-density configurations without changing the aircraft's fundamental dimensions.

American's newer A321neos therefore benefit from a different layout that accommodates additional economy seating. The difference is also reflected in aircraft classification. American's newer aircraft are identified as A321-200NX models, while the former Alaska aircraft are listed as A321-200Ns. Although the distinction might appear relatively minor, it represents a meaningful physical difference that American has had to accommodate.

The latest data from ch-aviation shows that today, Alaska operates the following fleet.

American began introducing the former Alaska A321neos into passenger service from Miami International Airport (MIA) in spring 2024. Rather than immediately undertaking a complete interior refurbishment, the airline initially retained the aircraft's existing Alaska cabin configuration. This allowed the aircraft to enter service while preserving much of the arrangement they had operated with previously. The original layout provided 190 seats, divided between 16 seats in first class and 174 seats in the main cabin. Compared with American's more conventional A321neo configuration, this meant fewer first-class seats but generally more space throughout the passenger cabin. For travelers, the difference was not necessarily negative, as the original arrangement offered a relatively spacious experience. However, retaining the inherited interior was not a permanent solution for an airline operating a large fleet with established cabin standards. American has spent considerable effort standardizing its narrowbody interiors through its Oasis refurbishment program, which introduced consistent seating arrangements and onboard features across numerous aircraft. The former Alaska A321neos would eventually need to undergo a similar transformation.

The first aircraft to receive the Oasis interior was N950XV, which underwent its refurbishment in San Antonio. The project demonstrated that American could bring the aircraft closer to its existing onboard standards, but it could not completely eliminate the differences created by their original construction. The aircraft's exit-door arrangement would continue to determine how many seats could be installed.

The A320 family has gone from strength to strength.

Following their refurbishment, the 10 former Alaska aircraft adopted a 190-seat configuration consisting of 20 first-class seats and 170 economy seats. This brought their premium-cabin capacity into line with American's standard arrangement, while retaining a smaller total number of seats than the airline's newer A321neos. The result is a cabin that looks more familiar but remains structurally different. American's regular A321neo configuration accommodates 196 passengers, including 20 first-class seats and 176 main-cabin seats. The additional six economy seats are possible because these aircraft have the newer Cabin Flex exit arrangement. The 10 inherited aircraft cannot simply receive the same number of seats because their original door positioning restricts the available cabin space.

This creates an interesting situation for American, which effectively operates two different versions of the same aircraft under one fleet umbrella. Both provide the benefits associated with the A321neo, including modern engines and improved fuel efficiency, but their interiors have different seating capacities and exit-row locations. From an operational perspective, this is an additional configuration for the airline to manage. The difference is particularly relevant because the 10 aircraft are not isolated examples that can be easily distinguished by passengers. They operate within the same broader A321neo fleet, and travelers may encounter either version when booking a flight. Although the cabin refurbishment has made the aircraft look more consistent, the underlying structural differences remain impossible to overlook when examining their seat maps.

Perhaps the most noticeable consequence of the unusual fleet arrangement concerns seat selection. American uses a single fleet code for both A321neo configurations, meaning passengers may not immediately realize which version they will be flying. However, the location of the emergency exits is different, creating two distinct seat maps despite the shared aircraft designation. The standard 196-seat A321neo has exit rows at rows 17 and 27, while on the 10 former Alaska aircraft, the corresponding exit rows are located at rows 11 and 24. Consequently, a passenger who selects an exit-row seat based on familiarity with one configuration could discover that the same row number has an entirely different purpose on the aircraft actually assigned to their flight. This can be particularly frustrating for travelers who deliberately pay extra for additional legroom.

A passenger expecting an exit-row seat could instead end up in a standard economy seat, potentially close to the lavatories. The differences extend beyond exit locations, with certain rows also having narrower seats or missing seat positions because of the aircraft's different cabin arrangement. The aircraft registrations provide another way to distinguish the two groups. The 10 former Alaska A321neos carry registrations ranging from N950XV through N959XV, while the remaining aircraft belong to the newer configuration. Ultimately, American inherited aircraft with an important piece of aviation history, but their early-production design has left the airline with a significant configuration difference.

Libghostty 1.0

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

libghostty-vt 1.0 is here. libghostty is a portable, high-performance C library for terminal emulation, extracted directly from the core of Ghostty. The 1.0 release means both the library itself and the C ABI are now stable.

A little over a year ago, I wrote that libghostty is coming. At the time, the Zig module had just been merged, the C API didn't exist, and nobody but Ghostty was a consumer. Today, the C API is a complete terminal emulator, it runs in production for millions of people across hundreds of products, both free and commercial, and it has a real, enforced ABI stability policy.

This post is about how we got here and what 1.0 actually means.1

Since starting the Ghostty project in 2022, the most common question I've been asked is "why?". I already answered this in the libghostty is coming post, so feel free to read that if you wish. Today, I'd rather answer this by showing the broad industry impact libghostty has already had. I believe this strongly validates its existence.

A year ago, I used a bug in JediTerm, the terminal emulator in JetBrains IDEs, as an example of why terminal emulation is hard to get right. A year later, the terminal in JetBrains IDEs now runs on libghostty. Every IntelliJ, PyCharm, GoLand, etc. user now has a libghostty-powered terminal. Based on a report by JetBrains, that's over 12.5 million active users.

Neovim has an approved pull request replacing libvterm with libghostty-vt. Among other things, it closes a 10-year-old issue about reflowing scrollback on resize. The maintainers are waiting on a tagged release before merging so that distributions can package the dependency separately, and the release today should enable that.

KDE developers are experimenting with a Konsole replacement built on it. Vercel's Turborepo uses it to render task output in its TUI. devenv uses it to drive shells. Antithesis used it to fuzz TUI programs and found real bugs in btop and others. Ghostel brings it to Emacs. ghostty-web is the WASM build with an Xterm.js-compatible API. Commercial products like OrbStack embed it. And a whole wave of new terminals and "agentic" coding tools, such as herdr and cmux, have been built on it rather than writing yet another emulator.

There are community bindings for Rust, Go, JavaScript, Swift, Python, .NET, Elixir, Dart, Java, and more, and the awesome-libghostty list tracks well over a hundred projects.

More people today use a terminal powered by libghostty than use Ghostty the application, by a very large multiple. And libghostty is having a much larger impact than being "just" a terminal emulator.2

1.0 means libghostty-vt follows semantic versioning and has strict ABI compatibility promises so that packagers across distributions and operating systems can be confident packaging libghostty-vt and program developers can be confident dynamically linking against it.

Beyond providing useful features, I believe the hallmark of a high-quality and enduring C library is ABI stability. So from the start, I took inspiration from C libraries I enjoyed working with and designed the API to have a strong, stable ABI that allows for future additions.

Every object is an opaque handle. The hundreds of values you might read or write go through a small set of enum-keyed getters and setters, so we can add new values easily without introducing a new symbol. Programs with newer headers linked against an older libghostty get GHOSTTY_UNSUPPORTED results instead of crashes or undefined behavior. The few non-opaque structs we have carry their size so that fields can be appended later, or contain reserved padding. Enums are pinned to int on every compiler, as much as the C standard allows.

Our CI enforces our ABI policy. Every change is checked with libabigail against a committed baseline on Linux, and against golden type manifests on macOS and WebAssembly.

On ELF platforms, every exported symbol is also versioned, so the dynamic loader refuses to load a mismatched library by name rather than failing in some mysterious way later.

In addition to ABI stability, the 1.0 release brings along the proper build tooling, tarballs, dependency licensing manifests, documentation, and more to enable every major operating system and distribution to package libghostty-vt through standard official tooling.

Libghostty has its own release cycle, separate from Ghostty the application. And it has its own tarballs with only the files and dependencies libghostty needs, resulting in smaller sizes and simpler licensing verification and compatibility.

Each release publishes a signed source tarball along with a companion dependencies tarball containing every Zig package in Zig's own cache layout, so a distribution can build the library completely offline. The build installs the shared and static libraries with a proper SONAME, headers, pkg-config files, and a CMake package config, and it honors --prefix, DESTDIR, and lib64. Debug info is kept by default so distros can split it out. All of this is documented in PACKAGING-LIBGHOSTTY.md, which ships with the tarball.

libghostty-vt is MIT licensed. At runtime it needs nothing beyond libc, and it can be built fully freestanding if you don't want that either.3

The library compiles in a small number of permissively licensed dependencies: simdutf and Google Highway for SIMD, a compact Unicode table for grapheme data, and Wuffs for image decoding in the Kitty graphics protocol. Both simdutf and Highway are built without libc++, so there is no C++ standard library dependency.

There is no copyleft code anywhere in the dependency tree. Every dependency is listed in a machine-readable manifest with its version, SPDX license, and hash, specifically so distros can fill in their bundled-library metadata more easily.

If a distro would rather dynamically link the system simdutf or Highway, there's a build flag for that.

libghostty-vt is highly portable. The table below lists all the platforms and architectures that we build and test (full unit tests plus running all examples) in CI:

There is a second set of platforms we build in CI but do not run tests for. These systems all have known users; we just haven't set up the machinery for automated testing:

Each of the tested platforms gets the artifacts it expects:

Portability isn't just about the ability to build and run. It's also about resource usage and being able to work in a wide variety of resource environments.

For resource usage, the library creates no threads, allocates only through an allocator you can customize, and uses minimal compute and memory. I've run 10,000 active terminals, each with a live shell producing output, on a Linux server with 4 cores and 8 GB of RAM. In this test, I had to use dash because bash used far more RAM than libghostty-vt and wouldn't fit into the 8 GB budget.

You can now create a terminal in ~1,500 lines of C that has more features and better performance than just about any terminal that existed in 2025, and most today in 2026.5 And thanks to the rich ecosystem of language bindings, you can do this in every major programming language. You get this for free. For almost no effort. You can just do it.

libghostty-vt is a generalized library, so we will focus on stability while implementing as many terminal protocols as exist (giving options to enable/disable them, of course) so that embedders have the luxury of choice.

Every bug any libghostty-vt embedder reports makes everyone else in the ecosystem better. Every feature we ship in libghostty-vt becomes available to everyone quickly and safely. Terminals can move faster with less fragmentation.

Thank you to everyone who contributed to the library, wrote bindings, filed bugs, and shipped something on top of it while the API was still experimental. As with all open source, this was the work of a large community.

Docs, examples, and downloads are at lib.ghostty.org.

As usual, I hate that I have to do this for every post, but I want to note this post was completely hand-written with no AI assistance. ↩

The numbers are rough because most consumers don't report usage, but JetBrains alone reports 12.5 million recurring active users, and that's before counting everything else on the list. ↩

In the original post I wrote "not even libc!" The default build does link libc, because the SIMD paths use it and the default allocator is malloc. Turn off SIMD and the library is freestanding, which is how it runs on bare-metal RISC-V and ARM targets. ↩

The feature flags aren't WASM-specific; they work on every target. They're likely useful for things like embedded devices too. ↩

"Features" here means the set of terminal protocols and sequences understood, and "performance" means parsing throughput and memory usage, since those are the parts libghostty-vt provides. ↩

The professions Americans trust the most — and least

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Doctors, scientists, and teachers earn the most trust in a 31-country survey, while politicians and influencers sit at the bottom of 20 groups.

A patient fills a prescription on a doctor's word. A parent signs a field trip form because a teacher vouched for the outing. A voter hears a campaign pledge and weighs whether to believe it. Each of these moments turns on a quiet judgment about whether the person on the other side deserves credence. Professionals shape the health care people receive, the schooling their children get, the money they save, the laws they live under, and the news they read. Most people cannot check that work for themselves. Few patients can audit a diagnosis, and few savers can inspect a bank's books. Faith in expertise fills the space between what people know and what they must accept, and it steers everyday choices large and small.

Public confidence splinters across these roles. Some occupations command broad goodwill, and others draw open suspicion. The divide runs through single fields and single institutions, where two jobs that share a label can land far apart in public esteem. A broad tag such as "business" or "government" can hide those splits, and anyone reading the popular mood through one catchall word risks missing them. Approval and doubt coexist. A single job can win praise from one slice of the population and scorn from another, and a tally of supporters on its own misses the balance between the two camps. Measuring how far trust outruns distrust, or the reverse, tells a clearer story about where each line of work stands.

Visual Capitalist ranked 20 professions and groups by that balance, using data from the Ipsos Global Trustworthiness Index 2026. Ipsos polled 23,537 adults online across 31 countries, with fieldwork running from June 19 to July 3, 2026. Respondents judged whether each profession or group in their own country was trustworthy or untrustworthy. Net trust, the measure behind the ranking, subtracts the share who chose untrustworthy from the share who chose trustworthy and expresses the result in percentage points. A group that 50% of respondents call trustworthy and 30% call untrustworthy, for example, would earn a net score of +20. The figure captures the margin of trust over distrust. It does not represent the share of people who trust the group. A positive score shows trust outweighing distrust, and a negative score signals the opposite. Each of the 31 countries carries equal weight in the averages, so the results do not form a global estimate scaled to population. The published figures leave the countries unnamed and offer no results for any one of them.

Doctors claim the highest net trust of all 20 groups in the survey, at +43 points. They belong to the health and knowledge category, a cluster that also takes in scientists, teachers, and personal trainers. Among adults polled across 31 countries, no other occupation drew a wider margin of confidence over doubt.

A 91-point gulf separates doctors from politicians, who post -48 at the opposite end of the list. Visual Capitalist calls the distance stark. No two groups stand farther apart, so the doctor-politician pairing marks the full width of public opinion the index captures.

Skepticism toward politicians runs deeper than goodwill toward doctors. Politicians sink 48 points below zero, and doctors climb 43 points above it. The bottom figure outweighs the top one by five points in magnitude, which suggests the survey's harshest verdict carries more force than its warmest endorsement.

Doctors hold a 27-point margin over the armed forces at +16. Every group outside the health and knowledge category, from judges and police to bankers and journalists, falls behind physicians by at least that distance.

Personal trainers, a fellow health and knowledge group, register +10, a full 33 points beneath doctors. A shared category label does not guarantee a shared standing with the public. The health label cannot account for the doctors' edge, because a group in the same field lags them by a wider gap than the one separating doctors from the armed forces.

Scientists hold a net trust score of +42 points, one point behind doctors and second among the 20 groups in the survey. A gap of a single point at the head of the list amounts to a near tie. Adults across the 31 countries rate researchers almost level with the physicians who treat them.

Doctors, scientists, and teachers fill the three highest positions in 27 of the 31 countries surveyed, according to Ipsos. Four countries depart from that order. The trio's grip across so many countries shows the standing of scientists does not hinge on a few outliers in the combined average.

Teachers, the third member of that trio, trail scientists by five points at +37. The one-point margin separating scientists from doctors is a fifth the size of that cushion. The summit of the list plays out as a two-way contest, with teachers a step back.

Scientists stand 90 points above politicians, the group with the lowest score of the 20. Nearly the full spread of opinion in the survey separates the people who conduct research from the people who seek elected office.

This slide runs about 185 words, below the 300-word minimum. The source supplies only the scientists' +42 net trust score, their second-place position, and the Ipsos finding that doctors, scientists, and teachers hold the top three spots in 27 of 31 countries. It gives no reasons, country names, demographics, or trends for scientists, and no approved web detail covers scientists, so the slide stops where the evidence ends instead of padding.

Vocabulary flag: "trust," "points," "scientists," and "countries" recur across paragraphs because no precise synonym exists for the measure, the subject, or the survey scope.

Teachers carry net trust of +37 points, good for third among the 20 groups, and they finish 21 points ahead of the armed forces at +16. No other pair of neighbors on the list sits so far apart. Each remaining step between adjacent groups in the top 10 covers six points or fewer. The steepest drop near the bottom, from clergy/priests at -11 to advertising executives at -26, spans 15 points and falls short of the teacher divide. Advertising executives and government ministers, 11 points apart, form the next-widest pair.

The step down from teachers covers more ground than the entire climb from the first negative score, held by pollsters at -2, up to the armed forces. The 18-point stretch between those marks holds seven groups, counting both ends, including personal trainers, tech entrepreneurs, judges, police, and lawyers. Police and pollsters, whose scores straddle zero, sit seven points apart, one-third the size of the gap below teachers.

Teachers trail doctors by six points and scientists by five. The three Health and knowledge groups fit inside a six-point band at the summit. Below them, the armed forces post a score less than half the mark teachers earn. Teachers outscore the combined total of the three groups beneath them. The armed forces, personal trainers, and tech entrepreneurs add up to +31, six points under the teacher figure.

Shared category membership offers no guarantee of a high finish. Personal trainers, the fourth Health and knowledge group, land at +10, which leaves them 27 points behind teachers. The gulf between teachers and politicians, who sit at -48, stretches to 85 points. Government ministers register -37, the exact inverse of the teacher score. Educators enjoy a margin of confidence as wide as the margin of doubt ministers face.

Read together, the gaps describe a list that splits into a leading trio and everyone else, with teachers guarding the boundary.

The armed forces hold net trust of +16 points, the strongest showing of any group outside the health and knowledge category. Doctors, scientists, and teachers, the only groups above the military, all work in medicine, research, or education. The military is the first public institution on the list, in fourth place, and the only group beyond health and knowledge to reach double digits.

The armed forces open a wide margin over the three other groups that share the public institutions label. Judges and police each reach +5, leaving the military 11 points clear of both. Government officials and civil servants slip to -9, 25 points below the armed forces' mark. Across those four groups, the category stretches from solid confidence to outright doubt, with the uniformed service at its upper end. The spread shows respondents sizing up each arm of the state on its own terms, from the barracks to the government office.

The military's edge holds against every sector outside health and knowledge. Tech entrepreneurs, the best-rated business and finance group, post +6, 10 points short of the armed forces. Lawyers, the highest-scoring professional services group, sit at 0. TV news anchors, the top media role, fall to -3. Clergy/priests, the lone religious group, register -11. Politicians, at -48, trail the armed forces by 64 points. In every one of those sectors, the best entry lands nearer to zero than to the military's mark.

Gallup reports that U.S. adults in its polling voiced more confidence in military veterans than in the military as an institution. The finding hints that views of the armed forces can hinge on whether people picture the organization or the individuals who served in it. Gallup's polling stays within the U.S., so it sheds light on one country's view of its military without speaking for the other countries in the wider survey.

Personal trainers carry a net trust score of +10, which hands the Health and knowledge category four of the five highest spots among the 20 groups surveyed. Doctors, scientists, and teachers claim the other three, and the armed forces stand as the sole group from outside that category to break into the upper tier.

Shared membership hides a steep internal drop. Trainers trail doctors by 33 points and teachers by 27, so they share a label with the most trusted professions without sharing their standing. The armed forces, at +16, sit six points above trainers, a gap less than a fifth of the one separating trainers from doctors.

Trainers build their work around individual attention. Personal fitness trainers assess a client's present fitness level, goals, and skills, then design individualized programs and track how the client progresses. Sessions may involve one client or a small group and can take place at a gym or in a client's home. Certification serves as a common entry requirement before trainers take on clients or coach members of a gym or health club. Employers favor certified candidates, though requirements differ from one facility to the next. Many trainers working in gyms and fitness facilities sell training sessions to members.

Trainers finish above every group in Business and finance, Professional services, Media, Religion, and Politics, along with three of the four Public institutions groups. Their cushion over the nearest of those groups stays slim. They clear tech entrepreneurs by four points and judges and police by five. Their +10 puts them 10 points above lawyers, who rest at zero. At the far end, trainers finish 58 points above politicians, who post the weakest score of the 20 groups. Among the four Health and knowledge groups, trainers post the sole score that falls nearer to zero than to the top mark.

Tech entrepreneurs hold net trust of +6 points, making them the single group in the business and finance category where trust outweighs distrust. Business leaders, bankers, and advertising executives all finish below zero. The nearest of them, business leaders, trails tech entrepreneurs by 14 points.

Adults surveyed across the 31 countries split the corporate world at the break-even mark. Founders of technology companies and the executives who run firms both earn their living in commerce, yet the two groups sit on opposite sides of the line. The ranking's authors flag wide gaps between jobs that share an industry, and business and finance offers a clear case. The business label on its own predicts little about where any one of its jobs will land.

Tech entrepreneurs share their stretch of the scale with public institutions and fitness work. Judges and police each post +5, one point lower, and personal trainers sit four points higher at +10. The sixth-place result leaves tech founders closer to the courts and law enforcement than to any fellow business group.

Their edge over distrust stays narrow. Tech entrepreneurs carry the slimmest margin among the six groups above +5. Doctors, scientists, teachers, the armed forces, and personal trainers all clear a wider gap between confidence and suspicion. Any reader weighing the business world's one positive score should see it as a modest win, far from the broad approval the top of the list enjoys.

Tech founders break a pattern among the groups with positive scores. Every other one comes from either health and knowledge or public institutions. No role in media, professional services, religion, or politics climbs above zero. Lawyers stop at zero without tipping into the plus column. Tech entrepreneurs stand as the one profession to earn net goodwill from outside public service and the health and knowledge fields.

Judges hold net trust of +5 points, a lead over their doubters narrower than that of any group above them on the 20-group list. Tech entrepreneurs, one spot higher, sit a single point ahead at +6. Personal trainers, a fitness occupation, draw a stronger result than the judiciary as well. The three health and knowledge groups at the summit enjoy a level of goodwill the courts do not approach. Judges outscore every media role in the survey, including TV news anchors, the strongest of the three. Respondents who call judges trustworthy outnumber the skeptics, yet the margin leaves the bench closer to the break-even line than to the top of the list.

Judges occupy the middle of the public institutions category, beneath the armed forces and ahead of the civil service. Public confidence in the state tips from approval to doubt somewhere between the courtroom and the government office. Uniformed and judicial roles stay in positive territory, while the officials who run day-to-day administration draw more suspicion than faith. The courts sit a long way from political leadership, with government ministers and politicians both among the three lowest scores on the list.

The two legal roles hug the line where trust and distrust cancel out, one a step above it and the other resting on it. Neither commands the broad support the health and knowledge groups receive, and together the two courtroom roles sit nearer neutral ground than firm public backing.

A poll estimates how people feel at one moment in time and makes no attempt to predict what comes next.

Courtney Kennedy of Ipsos suggested that anyone weighing a poll look for a named pollster with credentials and an open account of its methods. Stronger polls, she said, tend to lay out their procedures with greater clarity.

The 11-point slide from that business role to ministers marks the second-largest step between neighboring groups in the bottom half of the list. Only the gap separating clergy/priests from advertising executives runs wider. Every business and finance group, bankers and advertising executives included, earns a milder verdict than the people holding ministerial posts. Business leaders, at -8, stand 29 points ahead of ministers.

Each of the four public institutions in the survey finishes above ministers. The armed forces, judges, and police all hold positive scores, and ministers trail each by more than 40 points. The armed forces, at +16, sit 53 points higher. Judges and police, tied at +5, clear ministers by 42 points.

Politics claims two of the three spots at the foot of the list. Ministers share the category with politicians, who sit 11 points lower at -48. Social media influencers, at -44, land between the two political groups and trail ministers by seven points.

Ministers draw a harsher verdict than every media role except influencers. TV news anchors, the strongest media role at -3, rise 34 points above ministers, and journalists, at -10, sit 27 points clear. Clergy/priests, the lone religious group, hold a 26-point edge at -11.

Ministers sit 37 points below lawyers, whose score of zero marks the line where trust and distrust balance. Pollsters, one notch below that line at -2, outscore ministers by 35 points. The distance to doctors at the opposite end stretches to 80 points.

No other category in the table keeps every member below -30, so politics stands as the one field where skepticism reaches each role it contains.

Social media influencers post a net trust score of -44, the second-weakest result among the 20 professions and the poorest for any group outside politics. Only politicians land beneath them. Government ministers, the other political group near the bottom, finish 7 points higher at -37.

Influencers trail the other two media roles by a wide margin. TV news anchors score -3 and journalists -10, which leaves influencers 41 points behind anchors and 34 behind journalists. Visual Capitalist reads this spread as a sign of a major divide in how people view different media roles.

No other grouping in the survey stretches as far from its best member to its worst. Media jobs land at 11th, 14th, and 19th in the 20-group list. The government split between civil servants and ministers, the other sharp gap the source flags inside government, covers less ground than the distance separating anchors from influencers. Averaging the three roles into one media figure would mask the rift, landing at a number that describes none of them.

The influencer deficit runs more than four times as deep as the journalists' and more than 14 times as deep as the anchors'. Distrust outweighs trust for all three media roles, yet the shortfall for the two news jobs looks modest beside the hole influencers face. Anchors and journalists hover within reach of the break-even mark. Influencers, by contrast, sit within sight of the scale's floor.

Advertising executives, at -26 the lowest business and finance score, finish 18 points clear of influencers. Clergy/priests at -11 and bankers at -10 sit more than 30 points above them. Doctors, at +43, hold an 87-point edge. Every business group, every public institution, and the lone religious role outscore influencers.

Respondents place influencers nearer to politicians and ministers than to the anchors and journalists who share their field.

Politicians finish with a net score of -48 points, the weakest of all 20 groups, after 13% of respondents called them trustworthy against 61% who called them untrustworthy.

Skeptics outnumber believers by close to five to one. Each respondent who vouched for politicians stood alongside about 4.7 who dismissed them. The 61% figure also forms an outright majority, so more than half of everyone surveyed put politicians in the untrustworthy column. Politicians are the only group whose separate trustworthy and untrustworthy shares appear in the published results. Their -48 is the one score on the list readers can break into its two parts.

The two shares account for 74% of respondents. The other 26% landed in neither camp and offered no verdict either way. Those uncommitted respondents form the only pool politicians could win over without changing anyone's mind.

Politics posts the poorest average of the seven categories, at -42.5 across its two groups. Media comes next at -19 for its three roles, leaving the political category 23.5 points behind the second-worst field. Social media influencers account for most of that media deficit, pulling the category below the scores of TV news anchors and journalists, two roles that sit far closer to neutral. Politics has no such buffer. Its other group, government ministers, also draws heavy distrust. Influencers, at -44, still sit four points above politicians and form the politicians' closest neighbor on the list.

Politicians would remain in negative territory if every one of the uncommitted 26% swung to their side. Their trustworthy share would climb to 39%, leaving them 22 points shy of the 61% who distrust them. An even score would require 24% of respondents to cross from distrust to trust, leaving both shares at 37%. Any path to a positive score runs through people who have written politicians off.
